Appendix B Supporting Proofs
B.1 Proof of Lemma 1
Here, we follow the guidelines in (Conn et al., 2000, Theorem 7.2.1). Let us assume that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23≤ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\leq\xi. Then, there is a Lagrange multiplier ν∗\nu^{*} such that
ν∗​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ)=0⇔{ν∗=0,inactive constraint‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ,active constraint.\nu^{*}\>(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi)=0\Leftrightarrow\begin{cases}\nu^{*}=0,&\text{inactive constraint}\\
\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi,&\text{active constraint}.\end{cases}
(52)
(52)(\ref{eq:cscond}) is the unfolded Complementary Slackness (CS) condition Bertsekas (2017) for the constrained optimization problem (7). The active case occurs when 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} lies on the boundary of Ω\Omega, i.e., gξ​(𝐬∗)=0⇔‖𝐬∗‖23=ξg_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=0\Leftrightarrow\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi and the inactive case occurs when 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} lies in the interior of Ω\Omega, i.e. gξ​(𝐬∗)<0⇔‖𝐬∗‖23<ξg_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})<0\Leftrightarrow\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}<\xi.
⊳\rhd
Active constraint case.
We assume that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi. From the first-order optimality conditions Bertsekas (2017), there exists a Lagrange multiplier ν∗\nu^{*}, such that
∇𝐬Lξ​(𝐬∗,ν∗)=𝟎⇔∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗​∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)=𝟎⇔∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬∗+∇f​(𝐱k)⏟∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐬∗=𝟎⇔(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)​𝐬∗=−∇f​(𝐱k),\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*},\nu^{*})=\bm{0}\Leftrightarrow\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\nu^{*}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\bm{0}\\
\Leftrightarrow\underbrace{\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}^{*}+\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})}_{\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})}+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}\mathbf{s}^{*}=\bm{0}\Leftrightarrow\\
\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}^{*}=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),
(53)
where the identity ∇𝐬‖𝐬‖23=3​‖𝐬‖2​𝐬\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=3\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}\mathbf{s} was used for some 𝐬\mathbf{s}. Let 𝐬\mathbf{s} be a feasible point on the boundary of Ω\Omega, i.e., ‖𝐬‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi. The Taylor expansion of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) around the minimizer 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is
m^​(𝐬)=m^​(𝐬∗)+(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)+12​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​∇𝐬2m^​(𝐬∗)​(𝐬−𝐬∗).\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\>(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\>(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(54)
From the second line in (53), we also have
∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)=−ν2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐬∗.\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=-\frac{\nu}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{s}^{*}.
(55)
Given (55) and the fact that 𝐬\mathbf{s} and 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} are feasible points on the boundary of Ω\Omega, i.e., ‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ=‖𝐬‖23\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}, we have
(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)=−ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​𝐬∗=ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​(‖𝐬∗‖22−𝐬T​𝐬∗)=ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​[12​(ξ2/3+ξ2/3)−𝐬T​𝐬∗]=ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​[12​(‖𝐬∗‖22+‖𝐬‖22)−𝐬T​𝐬∗],(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=-\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}=\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\mathbf{s}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*})\\
=\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\left[\frac{1}{2}\>\left(\xi^{2/3}+\xi^{2/3}\right)-\mathbf{s}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}\right]=\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\left[\frac{1}{2}\>\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)-\mathbf{s}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}\right],
(56)
which implies
(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)=ν∗4​‖𝐬∗‖2​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​(𝐬−𝐬∗).(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\frac{\nu^{*}}{4}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(57)
Combining (54), (57), and ∇𝐬2m^​(𝐬∗)=∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) gives
m^​(𝐬)=m^​(𝐬∗)+14​ν∗​‖𝐬∗‖2​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​(𝐬−𝐬∗)+12​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​(𝐬−𝐬∗)=m^​(𝐬∗)+12​(𝐬−𝐬∗)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖​𝐈)​(𝐬−𝐬∗).\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{1}{4}\nu^{*}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\>(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})\\
=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\>(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert\>\mathbf{I}\right)(\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(58)
The second-order optimality condition Bertsekas (2017, Proposition 4.3.1) for 𝐳∈ℝd\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} yields
𝐳T​(∇𝐬2m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗​∇𝐬2gξ​(𝐬∗))​𝐳≥0,\mathbf{z}^{T}\Bigl(\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\nu^{*}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\Bigr)\mathbf{z}\geq 0,
(59)
where
∇𝐬2​m^​(𝐬∗)⏟∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗​∇𝐬2gξ​(𝐬∗)=(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)+ν∗2​𝐬∗​(𝐬∗)T‖𝐬∗‖2\underbrace{\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})}_{\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})}+\nu^{*}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}^{2}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\>\frac{\mathbf{s}^{*}(\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}}
(60)
such that 𝐳T​∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)=12​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐳T​𝐬∗=0⇔𝐳T​𝐬∗=0\mathbf{z}^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}\>\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}=0\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}=0. Since 𝐬∗≠𝟎\mathbf{s}^{*}\neq\bm{0}, we have
𝐳T​{(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)+ν∗2​𝐬∗​(𝐬∗)T‖𝐬∗‖2}​𝐳≥0\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{T}\left\{\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\>\frac{\mathbf{s}^{*}(\mathbf{s}^{*})^{T}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}}\right\}\mathbf{z}\geq 0
⇔\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow
(61)
𝐳T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)​𝐳+ν∗2​(𝐳T​𝐬∗)2‖𝐬∗‖2≥0\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{T}\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{z}+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\>\frac{(\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*})^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}}\geq 0
\displaystyle.
Using 𝐳T​𝐬∗=0\mathbf{z}^{T}\mathbf{s}^{*}=0 in (61) we get
𝐳T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)​𝐳≥0.\mathbf{z}^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{z}\geq 0.
(62)
This indicates that ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is positive semi-definite for vectors in the direction of the null-space of ∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*}), i.e., perpendicular to ∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*}).
It remains to consider vectors 𝐰∈ℝd\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} that do not belong to the null-space of ∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*}), i.e., 𝐰T​∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)≠0\mathbf{w}^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\neq 0, and prove that ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is also positive semi-definite. To this end, define the line 𝐬=𝐬∗+α​𝐰\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{*}+\alpha~\mathbf{w} as a function of α\alpha. Because we are interested in 𝐰\mathbf{w}, such that 𝐰T​∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)≠0\mathbf{w}^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\neq 0, the line intersects the constraint gξ​(𝐬)=0⇔‖𝐬‖23=ξg_{\xi}(\mathbf{s})=0\Leftrightarrow\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi in two values of α\alpha. For α=0\alpha=0 we have 𝐬=𝐬∗\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{*} and the aforementioned discussion holds. For α≠0\alpha\neq 0, 𝐬\mathbf{s} satisfies ‖𝐬‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi. In the latter case, we may write 𝐬−𝐬∗=α​𝐰\mathbf{s}-\mathbf{s}^{*}=\alpha~\mathbf{w}. From (58), we arrive at
m^​(𝐬)=m^​(𝐬∗)+α22​𝐰T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)​𝐰,\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\>\mathbf{w}^{T}\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{w},
(63)
with α≠0\alpha\neq 0. Given the assumption that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer, i.e., m^​(𝐬∗)≤m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}), (63) implies that ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is positive semi-definite. So far, we have shown that if
𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi, then ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is positive semi-definite either in the direction of the null-space of ∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*}) or not. Conversely, if ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is positive semi-definite, from (58) and (63), we arrive at m^​(𝐬∗)≤m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}), i.e., 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi.
Regarding the uniqueness of the solution, when ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I} is positive definite, from (58) and (63) we have that m^​(𝐬∗)<m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})<\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}), which indicates that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a unique minimizer subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi.
⊳\rhd
Inactive constraint case.
In this case, we assume that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23<ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}<\xi when ν∗=0\nu^{*}=0. From (53) we obtain
∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬∗=−∇f​(𝐱k).\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{s}^{*}=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}).
(64)
From the second-order optimality condition Bertsekas (2017, Proposition 4.3.1), it is implied that ∇𝐬𝐬2​Lξ​(𝐬∗,ν∗)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}\mathbf{s}}^{2}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*},\nu^{*}) is positive semi-definite. Using the latter fact, along with the fact that ∇𝐬𝐬2Lξ​(𝐬∗,ν∗)=∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla_{\mathbf{s}\mathbf{s}}^{2}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*},\nu^{*})=\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) when ν∗=0\nu^{*}=0, we get that ∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) is positive semi-definite. This, in turn, implies that we are dealing with a convex problem.
Conversely, when ∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) is positive semi-definite and ν∗=0\nu^{*}=0, we can use the Taylor expansion of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) in (54) along with the fact that
∇𝐬Lξ​(𝐬∗,ν∗)=𝟎\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*},\nu^{*})=\bm{0}
⇔∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗​∇𝐬gξ​(𝐬∗)0⏟as ν∗=0=𝟎\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\underbrace{\cancelto{0}{\nu^{*}\>\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{as $\nu^{*}=0$}\end{subarray}}=\bm{0}
(65)
⇔∇𝐬m^​(𝐬∗)=𝟎\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\bm{0}
to show that m^​(𝐬∗)≤m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}). This implies that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23<ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}<\xi. Regarding the uniqueness of the solution, when ∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) positive definite and ν∗=0\nu^{*}=0, we can solve (58) w.r.t. 𝐬∗=−∇2f(𝐱k)−1∇f(𝐱k)\mathbf{s}^{*}=-\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{-1}\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}), which indicates that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a unique minimizer subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23<ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}<\xi.
Given that no assumption has been made on the structure of ∇f​(𝐱)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}), we can repeat the aforementioned proof using Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})) instead of ∇f​(𝐱)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) to arrive at Corollary 3.
Corollary 3.
A vector 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) subject to ‖𝐬∗‖23≤ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\leq\xi if and only if satisfies
(Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈)​𝐬∗=−∇f​(𝐱k),\left(\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}^{*}=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),
(66)
Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈⪰0,\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\succeq 0,
(67)
and ν∗​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ)=0\nu^{*}\>(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi)=0, where ν∗≥0\nu^{*}\geq 0. If ∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬∗‖2​𝐈≻0\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}~\mathbf{I}\succ 0, then the minimizer 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is unique.
Corollary 3 will be used in the proof of Theorem 23.
B.2 Proof of Lemma 2
Starting from the primal optimization problem
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡Lξ​(𝐬,ν)=(8)min𝐬∈ℝd​‖𝐬‖22=τ⁡∇f​(𝐱k)T​𝐬+12​𝐬T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬+ν6​(τ3/2−ξ),\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lagrangeCubicOursEquiv})}}{{=}}\\
\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\tau\end{subarray}}\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\mathbf{s}+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}^{T}\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{s}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\tau^{3/2}-\xi\Bigr),
(68)
where ν\nu is the Lagrange multiplier, the optimal value of the primal problem can be expressed as
min𝐬∈ℝdLξ(𝐬,ν)=(8)min𝐬∈ℝd​τ≥0maxr∈Dν{∇f(𝐱k)T𝐬+12𝐬T∇2f(𝐱k)𝐬+ν6(τ3/2−ξ)+r​ν4(‖𝐬‖22−τ)},\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lagrangeCubicOursEquiv})}}{{=}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \tau\geq 0\end{subarray}}\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\Biggl\{\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\>\mathbf{s}+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\tau^{3/2}-\xi\Bigr)+\frac{r\nu}{4}\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\tau\right)\Biggr\},
(69)
where rr is the Lagrange multiplier associated to the constraint ‖𝐬‖22=τ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\tau (Boyd and Vandenberghe, 2004, Section 5.4). It is essential to highlight that the optimality conditions outlined in Bertsekas (2017, Proposition 4.2.1) explicitly require rr to belong to ℝ\mathbb{R}. However, rr is restricted to Dν\pazocal{D}_{\nu} for reasons that become apparent as the proof unfolds. If the weak duality property is applied to the right-hand side (RHS) of (69), we arrive at
min𝐬∈ℝdLξ(𝐬,ν)≥maxr∈Dνmin𝐬∈ℝd​τ≥0{∇f(𝐱k)T𝐬+12𝐬T∇2f(𝐱k)𝐬+ν6(τ3/2−ξ)+r​ν4(‖𝐬‖22−τ)}.\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\geq\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \tau\geq 0\end{subarray}}\Biggl\{\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\>\mathbf{s}+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}+\\
\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\tau^{3/2}-\xi\Bigr)+\frac{r\nu}{4}\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\tau\right)\Biggl\}.
(70)
From the first-order optimality condition Bertsekas (2017), the optimal value in the Left Hand Side (LHS) of (68) w.r.t. 𝐬\mathbf{s} is attained by 𝐬\mathbf{s} that satisfies ∇𝐬Lξ​(𝐬,ν)=𝟎\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},{\nu})=\bm{0}, i.e.,
(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν2​‖𝐬‖2​𝐈)​𝐬=−∇f​(𝐱k),ν≥0.\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}\>\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),\quad\nu\geq 0.
(71)
At this point, we note that (71) differs from (9), because the stationarity of Lξ​(𝐬,ν)\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},{\nu}) is studied w.r.t. 𝐬\mathbf{s} only. Denote the RHS of (70) as
ℒξ(𝐬,ν,r,τ)=∇f(𝐱k)T𝐬+12𝐬T∇2f(𝐱k)𝐬+ν6(τ3/2−ξ)+r​ν4(‖𝐬‖22−τ)=∇f(𝐱k)T𝐬+12𝐬T(∇2f(𝐱k)+ν​r2𝐈)𝐬+ν6(τ3/2−ξ)−r​ν4τ.\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu,r,\tau)=\>\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\>\mathbf{s}+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\tau^{3/2}-\xi\Bigr)+\frac{r\nu}{4}\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\tau\Bigr)\\
=\>\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\mathbf{s}+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}^{T}\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{s}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\tau^{3/2}-\xi\Bigr)-\frac{r\nu}{4}\tau.
(72)
We start with the case ν>0\nu>0. Solving ∂τℒξ​(𝐬,ν,r,τ)=0\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu,r,\tau)=0 w.r.t. τ\tau, we get
τ∗=r2,\tau^{*}=r^{2},
(73)
where r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}. Restricting rr in Dν\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, implies that r>0r>0, which in turn implies τ∗>0\tau^{*}>0, as ‖𝐬‖=τ∗\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert=\tau^{*}. If τ∗=0\tau^{*}=0, we have ‖𝐬‖=0\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert=0, which leads to the trivial solution, i.e., the zero vector. Solving ∇𝐬ℒξ​(𝐬,ν,r,τ)=𝟎\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu,r,\tau)=\bm{0} w.r.t. 𝐬\mathbf{s}, we arrive at
∇f​(𝐱k)=−(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬.\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})=-\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{s}.
(74)
For r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, we get from (74)
𝐬(ν,r)=−(∇2f(𝐱k)+ν​r2𝐈)−1∇f(𝐱k),ν>0,\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)=-\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),\quad\nu>0,
(75)
which implies the dependence of 𝐬\mathbf{s} on the variables ν\nu and rr. Restricting rr in Dν\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, we achieve the invertibility in (75) when ν>0\nu>0. Substituting (73) and (74) in (72), we get
ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)=−12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)−ν6​ξ−ν12​r3.\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)=-\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)-\frac{\nu}{6}\xi-\frac{\nu}{12}r^{3}.
(76)
Combining (70) and (76) we get for ν>0\nu>0
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡Lξ​(𝐬,ν)≥maxr∈Dν⁡ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r).\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\geq\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\>{\mathscr{L}}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r).
(77)
The derivative of (76) w.r.t. rr is
∂rℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)=ν4​(‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22−r2).\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)=\frac{\nu}{4}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-r^{2}\Bigr).
(78)
Thus, for any ν>0\nu>0, the optimal value in the RHS of (77) is attained for r∗∈Dνr^{*}\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu} that solves
(∂ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)∂r)r=r∗=0.\left(\frac{\partial{\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)}}{\partial r}\right)_{r=r^{*}}=0.
(79)
Using (78) in (79), we have
r∗=‖𝐬⁡(ν,r∗)‖2​for any​ν>0.r^{*}=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*})\right\rVert_{2}\>\text{for any}\>\nu>0.
(80)
Restricting r∗r^{*} in Dν\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, we avoid the trivial solution 𝐬⁡(ν,r∗)=𝟎\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*})=\bm{0} for any ν>0\nu>0. This restriction on rr in (69) is precisely due to its inclusion in Dν\pazocal{D}_{\nu}.
Using (74) in (8) we attain
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=−𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)+12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬​(ν,r)+ν6​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ν6​ξ.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=-\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\frac{\nu}{6}\xi.
(81)
Adding and subtracting the terms ν12​r3\frac{\nu}{12}r^{3} and ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}, we get
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=−𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)+12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬​(ν,r)+ν6​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ν6​ξ+(ν12​r3−ν12​r3)⏟0+(ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22−ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22)⏟0.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=-\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\\
-\frac{\nu}{6}\xi+\underbrace{\left(\frac{\nu}{12}r^{3}-\frac{\nu}{12}r^{3}\right)}_{0}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)}_{0}.
(82)
Next, using (76), with appropriate rearrangements we arrive at
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+ν12​r3+ν6​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+ν12​(r3+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−3​r​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22)=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+ν12​(‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2−r)2​(r+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2).\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{12}r^{3}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{12}\>\Bigl(r^{3}+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-3r\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\Bigr)\\
=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{12}\>\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}-r\right)^{2}\left(r+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right).
(83)
Then, using (78) for r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu} and ν>0\nu>0 we obtain
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+43​ν​(r+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)(r+‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)2​(∂rℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r))2.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)+\frac{4}{3\nu}\>\frac{\left(r+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)}{\left(r+\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)^{2}}\Bigl(\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)\Bigr)^{2}.
(84)
When (79) is satisfied for some ν>0\nu>0, ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r) given by (84) is maximized w.r.t. r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}. From (84) we have
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r∗),ν)=maxr∈Dν⁡ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r).\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*}),\nu)=\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\>\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r).
(85)
In order to obtain (12), we need to show
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r∗),ν)=min𝐬∈ℝd⁡Lξ​(𝐬,ν),ν>0.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*}),\nu)=\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu),\quad\nu>0.
(86)
When r∗∈Dνr^{*}\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu} in (75) and using r∗=‖𝐬⁡(ν,r∗)‖2r^{*}=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*})\right\rVert_{2} for some ν>0\nu>0 in (80), we get
(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r∗)‖​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r∗)=−∇f​(𝐱k)\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*})\right\rVert\>\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*})=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})
(87)
which implies that 𝐬⁡(ν,r∗)\mathbf{s}(\nu,r^{*}) minimizes Lξ​(𝐬,ν)\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu).
We conclude with the case ν=0\nu=0. In this case, we observe that (12) is easily attained by applying (70) when equality holds, which concludes the proof.
B.3 Proof of Theorem 1
From the weak duality in (7) and (12) we have
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡maxν≥0​Lξ​(𝐬,ν)≥maxν≥0⁡min𝐬∈ℝd​Lξ​(𝐬,ν)=(12)maxν≥0⁡maxr∈Dν​ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)=maxr∈Dν,ν≥0⁡ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r),\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\nu\geq 0}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\geq\max_{\nu\geq 0}\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:minLmaxL})}}{{=}}\max_{\nu\geq 0}\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)=\max_{r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu},\nu\geq 0}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r),
(88)
where the last term in (88) refers to a joint optimization problem. We start with the case ν>0\nu>0. The derivative of (76) w.r.t. ν\nu is
∂νℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)=r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22−ξ6−r312,\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)=\frac{r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{\xi}{6}-\frac{r^{3}}{12},
(89)
where r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}.
To prove that (88) holds with equality and subsequently prove (17), we study the optimality conditions that maximize the RHS of (88). The optimal value in the RHS of (88) w.r.t ν>0\nu>0 is achieved by some ν∗>0\nu^{*}>0 that solves
(∂ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)∂ν)ν=ν∗=0,\left(\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)}{\partial\nu}\right)_{\nu=\nu^{*}}=0,
(90)
for r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}. In addition, the optimal value in the RHS of (88) w.r.t r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu} is achieved if (79) or equivalently (80) holds. Given that (80) holds for any ν>0\nu>0, without loss of generality, we assume that (80) also holds for ν∗>0\nu^{*}>0, i.e.,
r∗=‖𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)‖2​for any​ν∗>0.r^{*}=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})\right\rVert_{2}\>\text{for any}\>\nu^{*}>0.
(91)
When (91) holds, from (90) we get
ξ=3​r∗2​‖𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)‖22−r∗32.\xi=\frac{3r^{*}}{2}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})\right\rVert^{2}_{2}-\frac{{r^{*}}^{3}}{2}.
(92)
Solving (14) w.r.t. ∇f​(𝐱k)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) and substituting in (8) we get
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=−𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)+12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬​(ν,r)+ν6​(‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ξ).\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=-\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\\
\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\right).
(93)
Then, applying (92) for r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, we have
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=−𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)+12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​∇2f​(𝐱k)​𝐬​(ν,r)+ν6​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22+ν​r312.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=-\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\hskip 85.35826pt\\
\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\nu\>r^{3}}{12}.
(94)
Adding and subtracting ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2} we obtain
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=−12​𝐬​(ν,r)T​(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν​r2​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν,r)−ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22+ν6​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−ν​r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22+ν​r312.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=-\frac{1}{2}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}~\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)-\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\\
\frac{\nu\>r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\nu\>r^{3}}{12}.
(95)
Similarly adding and subtracting ν​ξ6\frac{\nu\xi}{6} and ν​r312\frac{\nu\>r^{3}}{12} reveals the term ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r) yielding
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=(13)ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+ν⁡(r312+ξ6−r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22)+ν12​(r3+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23−3​r​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22).\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lagrangianscr})}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)+\nu\left(\frac{r^{3}}{12}+\frac{\xi}{6}-\frac{r}{4}~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)+\hfill\frac{\nu}{12}\left(r^{3}+2\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-3r\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right).
(96)
The terms inside the first bracket of (96) are identified as −∂νℒξ(𝐬(ν,r),ν,r)-\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr), yielding
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)−ν​∂νℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+ν12​(‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2−r)2​(r+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)=(89)​ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+43​ν​(r+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)(r+‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)2​(∂rℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r))2−ν​∂νℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)-\nu~\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)+\frac{\nu}{12}\>\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}-r\right)^{2}\left(r+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)\\
\overset{(\ref{eq:partialLagrangnu})}{=}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)+\frac{4}{3\nu}\frac{\left(r+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)}{\left(r+\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)^{2}}\Bigl(\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)\Bigr)^{2}-\nu~\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)
(97)
and by rearranging terms, we arrive at
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν)=ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)−ν​∂νℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)+43​ν​(r+2​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)(r+‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2)2​(∂rℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r))2.\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu)=\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)-\nu~\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr)+\frac{4}{3\nu}\frac{\left(r+2~\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)}{\left(r+\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}\right)^{2}}\Bigl(\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r)\Bigr)^{2}.
(98)
When (79) and (90) hold, ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r)\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r\bigr) is maximized and from (98), we have
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗),ν∗,r∗)=maxν≥0,r∈Dν⁡ℒξ​(𝐬⁡(ν,r),ν,r),\pazocal{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*}),\nu^{*},r^{*}\bigr)=\max_{\nu\geq 0,r\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}}\>\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),\nu,r),
(99)
where r∗r^{*} and ν∗\nu^{*} optimize the RHS of (88). Given r∗r^{*}, ν∗\nu^{*}, and (99), to show (17), we need to prove
Lξ​(𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗),ν∗,r∗)=min𝐬∈ℝd⁡maxν≥0​Lξ​(𝐬,ν).\pazocal{L}_{\xi}\bigl(\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*}),\nu^{*},r^{*}\bigr)=\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\nu\geq 0}\>\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu).
(100)
To do so, we need to show that the optimal 𝐬\mathbf{s} in the RHS of (100) equals 𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*}) in the LHS of (100). The optimal 𝐬\mathbf{s} in the RHS of (100) satisfies Lemma 1. Thus, by Lemma 1, if 𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*}) satisfies the CS condition
ν∗​(‖𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)‖23−ξ)=0\nu^{*}\>(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi)=0
(101)
and the system of equations
(∇2f​(𝐱k)+ν∗2​‖𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)‖​𝐈)​𝐬​(ν∗,r∗)=−∇f​(𝐱k),\left(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})\right\rVert\>\mathbf{I}\right)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})=-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),
(102)
then (100) holds. (101) implies ‖𝐬⁡(ν∗,r∗)‖23=ξ\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu^{*},r^{*})\right\rVert_{2}^{3}=\xi for ν∗>0\nu^{*}>0, which is true because of (91). To prove (102), we apply (87), where without loss of generality we replace ν>0\nu>0 with ν∗>0\nu^{*}>0, and the proof is complete. Corollary 21, in the paper’s main body, summarizes this proof’s main result.
B.4 Proof of Theorem 2
The proof has two parts. The first part deals with the RHS of (22), while the second part deals with the LHS of (22). A similar procedure is followed to that in Kloft et al. (2009, Proposition 1) to prove Theorem 2.
⊳\rhd
First part.
Let 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} be the minimizer of (7) which satisfies the feasibility condition gξ​(𝐬∗)≤0g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\leq 0. We want to show that when M=ν∗M=\nu^{*}, 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is also a minimizer of (5). From Lemma 1, we recall that ν∗​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ)=0\nu^{*}\>(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi)=0. Consequently,
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡maxν≥0​Lξ​(𝐬,ν)=Lξ​(𝐬∗,ν∗)=m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗6​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ)0⏟0 from CS condition=m^​(𝐬∗).\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\nu\geq 0}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)=\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*},\nu^{*})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\underbrace{\cancelto{0}{\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{0 from CS condition}\end{subarray}}=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(103)
From Corollary 21, we have
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡maxν≥0​Lξ​(𝐬,ν)=maxν≥0⁡min𝐬∈ℝd⁡Lξ​(𝐬,ν)⏟ψ⁡(ν)=maxν≥0⁡ψ⁡(ν)=ψ⁡(ν∗),\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\nu\geq 0}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)=\max_{\nu\geq 0}\underbrace{\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu)}_{\psi(\nu)}=\max_{\nu\geq 0}\>\psi(\nu)=\psi(\nu^{*}),
(104)
where ψ⁡(ν)\psi(\nu) is the dual function of the constrained optimization problem (7). From (103) and (104) we have
m^​(𝐬∗)=ψ⁡(ν∗)=min𝐬∈ℝd⁡Lξ​(𝐬,ν∗)=min𝐬∈ℝd⁡{m^​(𝐬)+ν∗6​(‖𝐬‖23−ξ)⏞≤0,by feasibility}≤min𝐬∈ℝd⁡m^​(𝐬)=m^​(𝐬∗)+16​ν∗​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ)0⏟0 from CS condition=m^​(𝐬∗).\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=\psi(\nu^{*})=\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>\pazocal{L}_{\xi}(\mathbf{s},\nu^{*})=\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>\Bigl\{\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\overbrace{\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr)}^{\leq 0,\>\text{by feasibility}}\Bigr\}\\
\leq\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{1}{6}\underbrace{\cancelto{0}{\nu^{*}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr)}}_{\text{0 from CS condition}}=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(105)
Since the first and the last term in (105) are equal, due to the CS condition, the in-between inequalities hold with equality, i.e.,
min𝐬∈ℝd⁡{m^​(𝐬)+ν∗6​(‖𝐬‖23−ξ)}=m^​(𝐬∗)+ν∗6​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ).\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>\Bigl\{\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\left(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\right)\Bigr\}=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr).
(106)
Removing the constant term −ν∗6​ξ-\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\xi from both sides of (106), we obtain
min𝐬∈ℝdmν∗(𝐬)=m^(𝐬∗)+ν∗6‖𝐬∗‖23=(7)f(𝐱k)+∇f(𝐱k)T𝐬k∗+12𝐬∗T∇2f(𝐱k)𝐬∗+ν∗6‖𝐬∗‖23=(6)mν∗(𝐬∗),\min_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\>m_{\nu^{*}}(\mathbf{s})=\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{prob:cubicOursEquiv})}}{{=}}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})+\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})^{T}\mathbf{s}_{k}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{s}^{*}}^{T}\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{s}^{*}+\\
\frac{\nu^{*}}{6}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:basicCubicProblem})}}{{=}}m_{\nu^{*}}(\mathbf{s}^{*}),
(107)
which implies that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is also a minimizer of (5) with M=ν∗M=\nu^{*} and the first part of the proof is complete.
⊳\rhd
Second part.
Let 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} be a minimizer of (5). We should prove that 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is also a minimizer of (7) when ξ=‖𝐬∗‖23\xi=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}. For such ξ\xi, gξ​(𝐬∗)=0g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=0. We prove the second part by contradiction. Suppose, 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is not optimal in (7), i.e., there is a feasible point 𝐬\mathbf{s} such that m^​(𝐬)≤m^​(𝐬∗)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*}). For this feasible point we also have gξ​(𝐬)≤0g_{\xi}({\mathbf{s}})\leq 0 and gξ​(𝐬)≤gξ​(𝐬∗)g_{\xi}({\mathbf{s}})\leq g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*}). Then, we get,
m^​(𝐬)≤m^​(𝐬∗)⇔m^​(𝐬)+gξ​(𝐬)≤m^​(𝐬∗)+gξ​(𝐬∗)⇔m^​(𝐬)+ν6​(‖𝐬‖23−ξ)≤m^​(𝐬∗)+ν6​(‖𝐬∗‖23−ξ).\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\Leftrightarrow\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})+g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s})\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})\\
\Leftrightarrow\hat{m}(\mathbf{s})+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr)\leq\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}^{*})+\frac{\nu}{6}\>\Bigl(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}-\xi\Bigr).
(108)
Adding ν6​ξ\frac{\nu}{6}\xi in both sides of the last inequality in (108), using the definition of mM​(𝐬)m_{M}(\mathbf{s}) (6) with M=νM=\nu, and applying the definition of m^​(𝐬)\hat{m}(\mathbf{s}) (7), we get
mν​(𝐬)≤mν​(𝐬∗).m_{\nu}(\mathbf{s})\leq m_{\nu}(\mathbf{s}^{*}).
(109)
This is a contradiction, because 𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is a minimizer of (5). Hence,
𝐬∗\mathbf{s}^{*} is also a minimizer of (7), when gξ​(𝐬∗)=0⇔ξ=‖𝐬∗‖23g_{\xi}(\mathbf{s}^{*})=0\Leftrightarrow\xi=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}, which concludes the second part of the proof.
B.5 Proof of Lemma 5
We follow similar lines to the proof of (Kohler and Lucchi, 2017, Lemma 6 and Theorem 7). Note that Bkg\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g} is used instead of Bk\pazocal{B}_{k} to emphasize that the deviation bound in (44) and the sampling scheme in (45) are specifically derived using information associated with 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k}.
The proof resorts to Vector Bernstein’s inequality in Lemma 19 (discussed in Appendix B.13). Let us define the centered gradient
𝐳i,ks=∇fi​(𝐱k)−∇f​(𝐱k),\mathbf{z}_{i,k}^{s}=\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}),
(110)
where i=1,…,n=|Bkg|i=1,\dots,n=|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|. First, we show
‖𝐳i,ks‖2≤‖∇fi​(𝐱k)‖+‖∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≤2​Lf,\left\lVert\mathbf{z}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert+\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\leq 2L_{f},
(111)
which implies ‖𝐳i,ks‖22≤4​Lf2\left\lVert\mathbf{z}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 4L_{f}^{2}. Accordingly, σ2=Δ4​Lf2\sigma^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}4L_{f}^{2} in Lemma 19. In (111), we have used
‖∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≤1n​∑i=1n‖∇fi​(𝐱)‖2≤1n​∑i=1nLf=Lf,\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\nabla f_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{f}=L_{f},
(112)
where the triangle inequality and Assumption 1 have been applied. Then, we have
𝐳k=1|Bkg|​∑i=1n𝐳i,ks=𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k).\mathbf{z}_{k}=\frac{1}{|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{z}_{i,k}^{s}=\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}).
(113)
Using (113) in Lemma 19 for n=|Bkg|n=|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}| and σ2=4​Lf2\sigma^{2}=4L_{f}^{2} yields
Pr⁡(‖𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≥ϵ)≤exp⁡(−|Bkg|​ϵ232​Lf2+14).\Pr(\left\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\epsilon)\leq\exp\left(-|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{32L_{f}^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}\right).
(114)
Next, we require that the probability of the gradient deviation Pr⁡(‖𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≥ϵ)\Pr(\left\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\epsilon) is less than some δ∈(0,1]\delta\in(0,1], i.e.,
exp⁡(−|Bkg|​ϵ232​Lf2+14)≤δ⇔ϵ≥4​2​Lf​ln⁡1δ+14|Bkg|.\exp\left(-|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{32L_{f}^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\leq\delta\Leftrightarrow\epsilon\geq 4\sqrt{2}L_{f}\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{1}{4}}{|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|}}.
(115)
To derive (44), we use (115) in ‖𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≥ϵ\left\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\geq\epsilon, along with Assumption 28 to get
ϵ≤‖𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≥Cg​‖𝐬k‖22⇔4​2​Lf​ln⁡1δ+14|Bkg|≤Cg​‖𝐬k‖22,\epsilon\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\geq C_{g}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\Leftrightarrow 4\sqrt{2}L_{f}\sqrt{\frac{\ln\frac{1}{\delta}+\frac{1}{4}}{|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|}}\leq C_{g}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2},
(116)
which yields (45). Using the complementary probability
with δ∈(0,1]\delta\in(0,1], it is implied that
‖𝐠k−∇f​(𝐱k)‖2≤ϵ\left\lVert\mathbf{g}_{k}-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\epsilon
is fulfilled with high probability 1−δ1-\delta when (115) holds. The latter derives (44), and the proof is complete.
B.6 Proof of Lemma 6
Following similar lines to Kohler and Lucchi (2017, Lemma 8 and Theorem 9) and using BkH\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H} instead of Bk\pazocal{B}_{k} to emphasize that the deviation bound in (46) and the sampling scheme in (47) are obtained using information related to 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k}, we get
𝐁k=(30)1S​∑s=1S​Diag⁡(𝐇k​𝐯s⊙𝐯s)=1S​∑s=1S​Diag⁡((1|Bkg|​∑i∈Bkg∇2fi​(𝐱k))​𝐯s⊙𝐯s)=1S​∑s=1S​1|Bkg|​∑i∈BkgDiag⁡(∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s⊙𝐯s).\mathbf{B}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:approxHessianDiag})}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\pazocal{S}}\sum_{s=1}{S}\Diag\left(\mathbf{H}_{k}\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right)=\frac{1}{\pazocal{S}}\sum_{s=1}{S}\Diag\left(\left(\frac{1}{|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|}\sum_{i\in\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}}\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right)\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\pazocal{S}}\sum_{s=1}{S}\frac{1}{|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}|}\sum_{i\in\pazocal{B}_{k}^{g}}\Diag\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right).
(117)
Let
𝐁i,ks=Diag⁡(∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s⊙𝐯s).\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}=\Diag\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right).
(118)
For 𝐀∈ℝd×d\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} it is known that ‖𝐀‖2≤‖𝐀‖F\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{F} (Golub and Van Loan, 2012). Accordingly, for 𝐁i,ks\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s} we obtain
‖𝐁i,ks‖2=‖Diag⁡(∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s⊙𝐯s)‖2≤‖Diag⁡(∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s⊙𝐯s)‖F=∑j=1d([∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s]j​[𝐯s]j)2=∑j=1d([∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s]j)2,\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\Diag\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right)\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\Diag\left(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}\odot\mathbf{v}_{s}\right)\right\rVert_{F}\\
=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left([\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}]_{j}[\mathbf{v}_{s}]_{j}\right)^{2}}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left([\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}]_{j}\right)^{2}},
(119)
where [𝐯s]j=±1[\mathbf{v}_{s}]_{j}=\pm 1, yielding
‖𝐁i,ks‖2≤‖∇2fi​(𝐱k)​𝐯s‖2≤‖∇2fi​(𝐱k)‖2​‖𝐯s‖2.\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{2}.
(120)
For 𝐯s∈ℝd\mathbf{v}_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, ‖𝐯s‖2≤d​‖𝐯s‖∞\left\lVert\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\left\lVert\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{\infty} Gould et al. (1999),
where ‖𝐯s‖∞=max1≤i≤d⁡|[𝐯s]i|=1\left\lVert\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|[\mathbf{v}_{s}]_{i}|=1. This allows us to rewrite (120) as
‖𝐁i,ks‖2≤d​‖∇2fi​(𝐱k)‖2​‖𝐯s‖∞≤d​‖∇2fi​(𝐱k)‖2.\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{v}_{s}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{d}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}.
(121)
As a result
‖𝐁i,ks‖2≤d​Lg,\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}L_{g},
(122)
because ‖∇2fi​(𝐱k)‖2≤Lg\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{k})\right\rVert_{2}\leq L_{g} due to Assumption 1.
To apply the Matrix Bernstein’s inequality in Lemma 22, define the centered Hessian matrix
𝐙i,ks=𝐁i,ks−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k)),\mathbf{Z}_{i,k}^{s}=\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})),
(123)
where i=1,…,|BkH|i=1,\dots,|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|.
Let n′=|BkH|n^{\prime}=|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|. From Lemma 8, using Assumption 1, and applying the triangle inequality we have
‖Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))‖2≤‖∇2f​(𝐱)‖2≤1n′​∑i=1n′‖∇2fi​(𝐱)‖2≤1n′​∑i=1n′Lg≤Lg,\left\lVert\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}L_{g}\leq L_{g},
(124)
Using (122), (124), and applying triangle inequality yields
‖𝐙i,ks‖2=‖𝐁i,ks−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))‖2≤‖𝐁i,ks‖2+‖Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))‖2≤(d+1)​Lg,\left\lVert\mathbf{Z}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq(\sqrt{d}+1)L_{g},
(125)
which implies
‖𝐙i,ks‖2≤d​Lg,\left\lVert\mathbf{Z}_{i,k}^{s}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}L_{g},
(126)
as d≫1d\gg 1. Let

Again, we are interested in the probability OPENPr⁡(‖𝐁k−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))‖2)≥ϵ)\Pr(\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{k}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))\right\rVert_{2})\geq\epsilon) is less than some δ∈(0,1]\delta\in(0,1], i.e.,
2​d​exp⁡(−38​S​|BkH|​ϵd​Lg)≤δ⇔ϵ≥d​Lg​ln⁡2​dδS​|BkH|.2d\exp\left(-\frac{3}{8}\pazocal{S}|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{d}L_{g}}\right)\leq\delta\Leftrightarrow\epsilon\geq\sqrt{d}L_{g}\frac{\ln\frac{2d}{\delta}}{\pazocal{S}|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|}.

We have that for x≤1x\leq 1 that e−x≤e−x2e^{-x}\leq e^{-x^{2}}. (134) indicates how large ϵ\epsilon must be for the probability of a deviation in (133) to be at most δ\delta, depending on the number of Hutchinson samples S\pazocal{S}, the mini-batch size |BkH||\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|, the parameter dimension dd, and the smoothness constant LgL_{g}. Next, using (134) in Assumption 31, we get
ϵ≤‖𝐁k−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))‖2≤CB​‖𝐬k‖2⇔d​Lg​ln⁡2​dδS​|BkH|≤CB​‖𝐬k‖2⇔|BkH|≥d​Lg​ln⁡2​dδS​‖𝐬k‖2​CB,\epsilon\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{k}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq C_{B}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\Leftrightarrow\sqrt{d}L_{g}\frac{\ln\frac{2d}{\delta}}{\pazocal{S}|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|}\leq C_{B}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\Leftrightarrow|\pazocal{B}_{k}^{H}|\geq\sqrt{d}L_{g}\frac{\ln\frac{2d}{\delta}}{\pazocal{S}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}C_{B}},
(135)
which yields (47).
Using the complementary bound
Pr⁡(‖𝐁k−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))‖2≤ϵ)≥1−δ,\Pr(\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{k}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq\epsilon)\geq 1-\delta,
it is implied that
‖𝐁k−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))‖2≤ϵ\left\lVert\mathbf{B}_{k}-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq\epsilon
is fulfilled with high probability 1−δ1-\delta when (134) holds. The latter is (46) and the proof is complete.
B.7 Proof of Lemma 7
In the following, 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k} and 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} are utilized instead of ∇f​(𝐱k)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) and ∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}). This substitution is performed because 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k} and 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} are directly used in Algorithms 1 and 2, both of which operate on data batches. In this manner, 𝐬⁡(ν,r)\mathbf{s}(\nu,r) is now defined in terms of 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} and 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k}, rather than Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})) and ∇f​(𝐱k)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}), respectively.
To obtain the minimizer 𝐬⁡(ν,r)\mathbf{s}(\nu,r) in (20), we need to solve w.r.t. ν\nu and rr the system of equations
∂rℒξ​(ν,r)=0and∂νℒξ​(ν,r)=0.\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)=0.
(136)
The solution (ν,r)(\nu,r) in (136) can also be computed sequentially. We start with the case ν>0\nu>0. In this case, we can
first solve ∂rℒξ​(ν,r)=0\allowbreak\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)=0 w.r.t. r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}. Then, the optimal rr, can be used to solve ∂νℒξ​(ν,r)=0\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)=0 w.r.t. ν\nu to obtain the optimal ν>0\nu>0. Solving ∂rℒξ​(ν,r)=0\partial_{r}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)=0 w.r.t. rr, yields
ν4​{[−𝐠kT​(𝐁k+ν​r2​𝐈)−1]​[−(𝐁k+ν​r2​𝐈)−1​𝐠k]}−ν​r24=0⇔(18)ν⁡(‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22−r2)=0.\frac{\nu}{4}\>\Biggl\{\left[-\mathbf{g}_{k}^{T}\left(\mathbf{B}_{k}+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\right]\left[-\left(\mathbf{B}_{k}+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{k}\right]\Biggr\}-\frac{\nu\>r^{2}}{4}=0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:solutionh})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\\
\nu\>(\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-r^{2})=0.
(137)
For 𝐬⁡(ν,r)≠𝟎\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\neq\bm{0}, r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, and ν>0\nu>0. Fom (137), we obtain the root
r=‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2.r=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}.
(138)
Then, the optimal ν\nu can be computed by solving ∂νℒξ​(ν,r)=(13)0\partial_{\nu}\mathscr{L}_{\xi}(\nu,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:lagrangianscr})}}{{=}}0 w.r.t. ν\nu, i.e.,

(139)
which is rewritten as
r4​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22−ξ6−r312=0\frac{r}{4}\>\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}-\frac{\xi}{6}-\frac{r^{3}}{12}=0
(140)
for some ξ>0\xi>0. Substituting (138) in (140), the optimal rr is given by
r=ξ3.r=\sqrt[3]{\xi}.
(141)
Using (141) in (140), the optimal ν\nu can be computed by solving
ω⁡(ν,r)=‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2−ξ3=0,\omega(\nu,r)=\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}-\sqrt[3]{\xi}=0,
(142)
w.r.t. ν\nu for rr fixed. It is shown in Conn et al. (2000, Section 7.3.3) that instead of solving (142), it is more preferable to solve
ϕ⁡(ν,r)=1‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2−1ξ3=0.\phi(\nu,r)=\frac{1}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{\xi}}=0.
(143)
We conclude with the case ν=0\nu=0. In this case, the minimizer 𝐬⁡(ν,r)\mathbf{s}(\nu,r) in (20) is handled by Algorithm 2, which concludes the proof.
B.8 Preliminaries for Lemmata 6 and 9
Lemma 8.
For 𝐌∈ℝd×d\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} and Diag⁡(𝐌)\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M}) we have
‖𝐌‖2≥‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2.\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}.
(144)
Proof.
First, we prove
‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2=maxk⁡|Diag⁡(𝐌)k​k|,\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{k}|\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})_{kk}|,
(145)
Let d∗=maxk⁡Diag⁡(𝐌)k​kd^{*}=\max_{k}\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})_{kk}. Then, from the definition of the spectral norm, we have
‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2=max‖𝐱‖2=1⁡‖Diag⁡(𝐌)​𝐱‖2≤max‖𝐱‖2=1⁡∑k(Diag⁡(𝐌)k​k​𝐱k)2≤|d∗|​max‖𝐱‖2=1​∑k𝐱k2,\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\sqrt{\sum_{k}(\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})_{kk}\mathbf{x}_{k})^{2}}\leq|d^{*}|\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\sqrt{\sum_{k}\mathbf{x}_{k}^{2}},
(146)
which leads to
‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2≤|d∗|.\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}\leq|d^{*}|.
(147)
Let 𝐞m\mathbf{e}_{m} be the vector of all zeros except a 1 in the mmth position, where m=arg​maxi​Diag​(𝐌)iim=\argmax_{i}\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})_{ii}. Then,
‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2=max‖𝐱‖2=1⁡‖Diag⁡(𝐌)​𝐱‖2≥‖Diag⁡(𝐌)​𝐞m‖2,\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\mathbf{e}_{m}\right\rVert_{2},
(148)
which leads to
‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2≥|d∗|.\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2}\geq|d^{*}|.
(149)
(147) and (149) imply (145). From the definition of the spectral norm, we have
‖𝐌‖2=max‖𝐱‖2=1⁡𝐱T​𝐌T​𝐌𝐱‖𝐱‖22=max‖𝐱‖2=1⁡‖𝐌𝐱‖2‖𝐱‖2=max𝐱,𝐲≠𝟎⁡|𝐱T​𝐌𝐲|‖𝐱‖2​‖𝐲‖2≥|𝐞jT​𝐌𝐞i|=|𝐌i​j|.\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}=\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\sqrt{\frac{\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{M}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}}=\max_{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=1}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}}=\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\neq\bm{0}}\frac{|\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y}|}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}}\geq|\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{e}_{i}|=|\mathbf{M}_{ij}|.
(150)
Restricting (150) in the diagonal elements of 𝐌\mathbf{M} gives
‖𝐌‖2≥maxi⁡|Diag⁡(𝐌)i​i|,\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\max_{i}|\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})_{ii}|,
(151)
which using (145) leads to
‖𝐌‖2≥‖Diag⁡(𝐌)‖2,\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}\geq\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\mathbf{M})\right\rVert_{2},
(152)
which is (144). This inequality becomes an equality when 𝐌\mathbf{M} is a diagonal matrix. Therefore, (144) provides the tightest possible bound in this case.
∎
B.9 Lemmata 9 and 10
Given the Lipschitz continuity assumption of ∇2f​(𝐱)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}), Lemma 9 introduces the Lipschitz continuity of Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})). Lemma 9 is used in Lemmata 10 and 17. Lemma 10 is an adaptation of Nesterov and Polyak (2006, Lemma 1) tailored to fit the context of this analysis. Lemma 10 is used in Lemmata 14, 15, and 16.
Lemma 9.
If ∇2f​(𝐱)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}) is Lipschitz continuous in F\pazocal{F},
Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})) is also Lipschitz continuous, i.e.,
‖Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐲))‖2≤LH​‖𝐱−𝐲‖2.\left\lVert\operatorname{Diag}(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))-\operatorname{Diag}(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{y}))\right\rVert_{2}\leq L_{H}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}.
(153)
Proof.
The proof of the lemma is easily obtained by combining the Lipschitz continuity of the Hessian matrix in Assumption 1 (see also Remark 1) with Lemma 8 in Appendix B.8. In Lemma 8, we use 𝐌=∇2f​(𝐱)−∇2f​(𝐲)\mathbf{M}=\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})-\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{y}).
∎
Lemma 10.
For any 𝐱\mathbf{x} and 𝐲\mathbf{y} in F\pazocal{F}, we have
‖∇f​(𝐲)−∇f​(𝐱)−Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱))​(𝐲−𝐱)‖2≤LH2​‖𝐲−𝐱‖2\left\lVert\nabla f(\mathbf{y})-\nabla f(\mathbf{x})-\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{L_{H}}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}
(154)
and
|f(𝐲)−f(𝐱)−∇f(𝐱)T(𝐲−𝐱)−12(𝐲−𝐱)TDiag(∇2f(𝐱))(𝐲−𝐱)|≤LH6‖𝐲−𝐱‖23.\Bigl|f(\mathbf{y})-f(\mathbf{x})-\nabla f(\mathbf{x})^{T}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})^{T}\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}))(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\Bigr|\leq\frac{L_{H}}{6}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{3}.
(155)
Proof.
Nesterov and Polyak (2006, Lemma 1) does not make any assumption on the structure of ∇2f​(𝐱)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}). The only assumption to derive Nesterov and Polyak (2006, Lemma 1) is the Lipschitz continuity of ∇2f​(𝐱)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}). Thus, given Lemma 9 and following the proof guidelines in Nesterov and Polyak (2006, Lemma 1), (154) and (155) are easily derived, which concludes the proof.
∎
B.10 Details for Algorithm 2
Here, Algorithm 2 is discussed when 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} and 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k} are utilized instead of ∇f​(𝐱k)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}) and ∇2f​(𝐱k)\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k}), respectively. This is done because 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} and 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k} are directly involved in the application of Algorithm 1, which operates on data batches. In this manner, 𝐬⁡(ν,r)\mathbf{s}(\nu,r) and ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) are now defined with respect to 𝐁k\mathbf{B}_{k} and 𝐠k\mathbf{g}_{k}, rather than Diag⁡(∇2f​(𝐱k))\Diag(\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x}_{k})) and ∇f​(𝐱k)\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_{k}), respectively.
Lemma 11 provides some useful properties of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) exploited in line 21 of Algorithm 2. Lemma 11 is used in Lemma 12 which shows that for some r∈Dνr\in\pazocal{D}_{\nu}, Newton-Raphson updates in line 21 of Algorithm 2 converge to the roots of ϕ⁡(ν,r)=0\phi(\nu,r)=0 w.r.t. ν>0\nu>0.
However, the Newton-Raphson method may diverge on its own, and appropriate safeguards are necessary to prevent this. These safeguards are adopted from (Conn et al., 2000, Algorithm 7.3.6) in Algorithm 2. Lemma 176 stems from Conn et al. (2000, Lemma 7.3.5) adapted to the analysis here. Lemma 176 provides the termination rule used in Algorithm 2.
Lemma 11.
Let
𝐇~k​(ν,r)=Δ𝐁k+ν​r2​𝐈.\tilde{\mathbf{H}}_{k}(\nu,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\mathbf{B}_{k}+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}.
(156)
Suppose 𝐠k≠𝟎\mathbf{g}_{k}\neq\bm{0} and ν​r>max⁡{0,−2​λd​(𝐁k)}\nu\>r>\max\bigl\{0,\allowbreak-2\>\lambda_{d}\bigl(\mathbf{B}_{k}\bigr)\bigr\} for some ν>0\nu>0 and r>0r>0. Then, the function ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) is strictly increasing and concave w.r.t. ν\nu and fixed rr. The first- and second-order partial derivatives of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) w.r.t. ν\nu are
∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)=−∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r)‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23>0\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)=-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}}>0
(157)
and
∂ν2ϕ⁡(ν,r)=3​{(∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r))2‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖25−‖∂ν𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖25}≤0,\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu,r)=3\Bigg\{\frac{\bigl(\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\bigr)^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{5}}-\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{5}}\Bigg\}\leq 0,
(158)
respectively, with
∂ν𝐬⁡(ν,r)=−r2​𝐇~k−1​(ν,r)​𝐬​(ν,r).\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)=-\frac{r}{2}\>\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r).
(159)
Proof.
Following similar lines to Conn et al. (2000, Lemma 7.3.1), the first-order partial derivative of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) (51) w.r.t. ν\nu is
∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)=∂ν(𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r))−12−∂ν1ξ30=−12‖𝐬(ν,r)‖2−3∑ℓ=1d2∂ν(𝐬(ν,r))ℓ(𝐬(ν,r))ℓ=−∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r)‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23,\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)=\partial_{\nu}\left(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\cancelto{0}{\partial_{\nu}\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{\xi}}}\\
=-\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{-3}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}2\>\partial_{\nu}(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r))_{\ell}\>(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r))_{\ell}=-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}},
(161)
which is (157).
The second-order partial derivative of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) w.r.t. ν\nu reads
∂ν2ϕ(ν,r)=−∂ν{(𝐬(ν,r)T𝐬(ν,r))−32(∂ν𝐬(ν,r)T𝐬(ν,r))}={3​(∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r))2‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖25−∂ν2𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r)+‖∂ν𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖23}.\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu,r)=-\partial_{\nu}\Biggl\{\left(\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\left(\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right)\Biggr\}\\
=\Biggl\{3\frac{\bigl(\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\bigr)^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{5}}-\frac{\partial_{\nu}^{2}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)+\left\lVert\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{3}}\Biggr\}.
(162)
The first-order partial derivative of
𝐬⁡(ν,r)=(18)−𝐇~k−1​(ν,r)​𝐠k\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:solutionh})}}{{=}}-\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{g}_{k}
(163)
w.r.t. ν\nu is
∂ν𝐬⁡(ν,r)=r2​𝐇~k−2​(ν,r)​𝐠k=(163)−r2​𝐇~k−1​(ν,r)​𝐬​(ν,r),\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)=\frac{r}{2}\>\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-2}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{g}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:sx})}}{{=}}-\frac{r}{2}\>\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r),
(164)
which is (159). From (164) and the assumptions about ν\nu and rr, i.e., ν​r>max⁡{0,−2​λd​(𝐁k)}\nu\>r>\max\{0\allowbreak,-2\>\lambda_{d}\bigl(\mathbf{B}_{k}\bigr)\} with ν>0\nu>0 and r>0r>0, we obtain
∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r)=−r2​𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐇~k−1​(ν,r)​𝐬​(ν,r)<0.\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)=-\frac{r}{2}~\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)<0.
(165)
Using (165) in (161), we infer that ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) is strictly increasing w.r.t. ν\nu. The second derivative of (163) w.r.t. ν\nu is given by
∂ν2𝐬⁡(ν,r)=r22​𝐇~k−2​(ν,r)​𝐬​(ν,r).\partial_{\nu}^{2}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)=\frac{r^{2}}{2}~\tilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-2}_{k}(\nu,r)\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r).

From (163) and (166) we get

The substitution of (167) in (162) yields
∂ν2ϕ⁡(ν,r)=3​{(∂ν𝐬​(ν,r)T​𝐬​(ν,r))2‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖25−‖∂ν𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22​‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖22‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖25},\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu,r)=3\Bigg\{\frac{\bigl(\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)^{T}\>\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\bigr)^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{5}}-\frac{\left\lVert\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}^{5}}\Bigg\},

which is (158). in (168),
which completes the proof.

Lemma 12.
Let ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) satisfy Lemma 11. Suppose that for some ν>0\nu>0 and r>0r>0 we have ν​r>max⁡{0,−2​λd​(𝐁k)}\nu\>r>\max\left\{0,-2\>\lambda_{d}\bigl(\mathbf{B}_{k}\bigr)\right\} and ϕ⁡(ν,r)<0\phi(\nu,r)<0. Then for a fixed rr, the Newton iterates
ν+←ν−ϕ⁡(ν,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r),\nu^{+}\leftarrow\nu-\frac{\phi(\nu,r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)},
(169)
will still satisfy ϕ⁡(ν+,r)<0\phi(\nu^{+},r)<0 and convergence monotonically toward the root ν∗\nu^{*} of ϕ⁡(ν,r)=0\phi(\nu,r)=0 w.r.t. ν\nu. The convergence of the Newton iterations w.r.t. ν\nu is at least linear and ultimately quadratic.
Proof.
Following similar lines to Conn et al. (2000, Lemma 7.3.2), we study the convergence of the Newton iterations in (169) w.r.t. ν\nu when rr is fixed. Suppose that ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) satisfies Lemma 11, which implies ∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)>0\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)>0. Then, from the Newton iteration w.r.t. ν\nu in (169), we have
ϕ⁡(ν,r)+(ν+−ν)​∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)=0.\phi(\nu,r)+(\nu^{+}-\nu)\>\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)=0.
(170)
According to Lemma 11, ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) is concave, i.e., ∂ν2ϕ⁡(ν,r)≤0\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu,r)\leq 0. Combining the concavity of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) with (170) we get
ϕ⁡(ν+,r)<ϕ⁡(ν,r)+(ν+−ν)​∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)=0\phi(\nu^{+},r)<\phi(\nu,r)+(\nu^{+}-\nu)\>\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)=0
which proves that ϕ⁡(ν,r)<0\phi(\nu,r)<0 is inherited by all Newton iterations w.r.t. ν\nu. Let (ν∗,r)(\nu^{*},r) be the root of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r). In addition, let (νI,r)(\nu^{I},r) be an intermediate point between points (ν,r)(\nu,r) and (ν∗,r)(\nu^{*},r), i.e., (νI,r)=α⁡(ν,r)+(1−α)​(ν∗,r)(\nu^{I},r)=\alpha~(\nu,r)+(1-\alpha)\>(\nu^{*},r) with α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1). Then, the Taylor expansion about ν∗,r\nu^{*},r reads as
ϕ⁡(ν,r)=ϕ⁡(ν∗,r)0+∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)​(ν−ν∗)+12​∂ν2ϕ⁡(νI,r)​(ν−ν∗)2+O⁡((ν−ν∗)3).\phi(\nu,r)=\cancelto{0}{\phi(\nu^{*},r)}+\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)(\nu-\nu^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\>\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu^{I},r)(\nu-\nu^{*})^{2}+\pazocal{O}\left((\nu-\nu^{*})^{3}\right).
(171)
We assume that (ν,r)(\nu,r) is close to (ν∗,r)(\nu^{*},r). This proximity implies that the last term in equation (171) becomes even closer to zero, and consequently, it is omitted from the subsequent analysis. The assumption that (ν,r)(\nu,r) is in proximity to (ν∗,r)(\nu^{*},r) ensures the convergence of the Newton method (Bertsekas, 2017). This proximity is achieved by utilizing the safeguarded Newton Algorithm 2. A comprehensive analysis of the safeguarded Newton methodology can be found in (Conn et al., 2000).
Subtracting ν∗\nu^{*} from both sides of (169), i.e.,
ν+−ν∗=(ν−ν∗)−ϕ⁡(ν,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)\nu^{+}-\nu^{*}=(\nu-\nu^{*})-\frac{\phi(\nu,r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}
(172)
and substituting (171) in (172), we arrive at
ν+−ν∗=(1−∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r))​(ν−ν∗)−12​∂ν2ϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)​(ν−ν∗)2.\nu^{+}-\nu^{*}=\left(1-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}\right)(\nu-\nu^{*})-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial_{\nu}^{2}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}(\nu-\nu^{*})^{2}.
(173)
We examine the following cases:
1.
If |1−∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)|>1\Bigl|1\allowbreak-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}\Bigr|>1, (173) diverges.
2.
If |1−∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)|<1\Bigl|1\allowbreak-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}\Bigr|<1, we have at least linear convergence in (173).
3.
If |1−∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)|=0\Bigl|1-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}\Bigr|=0, we have quadratic convergence as the linear term vanishes in (173).
From the concavity of ϕ⁡(ν,r)\phi(\nu,r) w.r.t. ν\nu, we have that ∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r) is decreasing, which implies that |1−∂νϕ⁡(νI,r)∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)|<1\Bigl|1-\frac{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu^{I},r)}{\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)}\Bigr|<1. Thus, the Newton iterations w.r.t. ν\nu in (169) converge at least linearly and ultimately quadratically, which completes the proof.
∎
Remark 3.
Lemma 11 implies that ∂νϕ⁡(ν,r)>0\partial_{\nu}\phi(\nu,r)>0. Suppose that for some ν\nu and rr we have ϕ⁡(ν,r)<0\phi(\nu,r)<0, i.e., Lemma 12 holds. Then, by (169) we have that ν+>ν\nu^{+}>\nu. Given ν+>ν\nu^{+}>\nu and the initial values of rr and ν\nu in lines 2, 4, and 7 of Algorithm 2, the optimal rr and ν\nu always satisfy 𝐁k+ν​r2​𝐈≻𝟎\mathbf{B}_{k}+\frac{\nu\>r}{2}\>\mathbf{I}\succ\bm{0}.

For some ν>0\nu>0 and r>0r>0, suppose ν​r>max⁡{0,−2​λd​(𝐁k)}\nu\>r>\max\{0,-2\>\lambda_{d}(\mathbf{B}_{k})\} and
|‖𝐬⁡(ν,r)‖2−ξ13|≤κeasy​ξ13|\left\lVert\mathbf{s}(\nu,r)\right\rVert_{2}-\xi^{\frac{1}{3}}|\leq\kappa_{\text{easy}}~\xi^{\frac{1}{3}}
